Proof 15.79

(Proof of Lemma
11.1
.)
.

It clearly suffices to prove that
\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\nu\right]=0

for any partition
\nu
that does not satisfy
\nu\subseteq\lambda
. But this
is obvious, because if
\nu
does not satisfy
\nu\subseteq\lambda
, then the
set
\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)
is empty (by Lemma

2.8
, applied to
\mu=\nu
), and thus we have

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\nu\right]=\sum\limits_{D\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in D}\left(x_{i}+y_{j}\right)=\left(\text{empty sum}\right)=0.

Proof 15.80

(Proof of Lemma
11.2
.)
.

Lemma
10.4
yields that the partitions
\nu
that satisfy

\mu\lessdot\nu
are precisely the partitions
\mu^{+k}
for the elements

k\in\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)
. Hence,

\sum\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\nu\right]=\sum\limits_{k\in\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right].

(161)

(since the partitions
\mu^{+k}
for different
k\in\operatorname{ER}\left(\mu\right)
are furthermore distinct).

Recall that
\mu_{n}=0
, so that
\mu_{j}=0
for all
j\geq n
.
Hence, every integer
k>n
satisfies
\mu_{k-1}=\mu_{k}
(since

\mu_{k-1}=0
and
\mu_{k}=0
) and thus
k\notin\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)
. In other words, every integer in
\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)
is
\leq n
. In other words,
\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)\subseteq\left[n\right]
. However,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
k\in\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{k\in\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)}\\
\text{(since }\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)\subseteq\left[n\right]\text{)}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
k\notin\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(by the definition of
}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]\text{,}\\
\text{since }k\notin\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)\text{ shows that }\mu^{+k}\\
\text{is not a partition)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k\in\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right].

Comparing this with (
161
), we find

\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]=\sum\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\nu\right]=\sum\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\nu\right]

(by Lemma
11.1
). This proves Lemma

11.2
.

Proof 15.81

(Proof of Lemma
11.3
.)
.

Let
\mathbf{b}^{\ast}=\left(b_{1}^{\ast},b_{2}^{\ast},b_{3}^{\ast},\ldots\right)
be the flagging
induced by
\lambda/\mu^{+k}
.

(a)
Assume that
m_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
.

We are in one of the following two cases:

Case 1:
We have
k\in\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)
.

Case 2:
We have
k\notin\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)
.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case, we have
k\in\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)
. Hence,
\mu^{+k}
is a partition.

For each
i\geq 1
, we have
b_{i}^{\ast}=b_{i}
(by Lemma
10.14

(a)
, since
m_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
) and
\left(\mu^{+k}\right)_{i}=\mu_{i}+\left[k=i\right]
(by Lemma
10.5
).

However,
\mu^{+k}
is a partition, and
\mathbf{b}^{\ast}=\left(b_{1}^{\ast},b_{2}^{\ast},b_{3}^{\ast},\ldots\right)
is the flagging induced by

\lambda/\mu^{+k}
. Thus, (
72
) (applied to
\mu^{+k}
,
\mathbf{b}^{\ast}
and

b_{i}^{\ast}
instead of
\mu
,
\mathbf{b}
and
b_{i}
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]

\displaystyle=\det\left(h\left(\underbrace{\left(\mu^{+k}\right)_{i}}_{=\mu_{i}+\left[k=i\right]}-i+j,\ \ \underbrace{b_{i}^{\ast}}_{=b_{i}},\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}

\displaystyle=\det\left(h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\left[k=i\right],\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

Thus, Lemma
11.3

(a)
is proved in Case 1.

Let us now consider Case 2. In this case, we have
k\notin\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)
. Hence,
k\neq 1
and
\mu_{k-1}=\mu_{k}
. Thus, Lemma

10.13

(a)
(applied to
j=k
) yields
b_{k}=b_{k-1}
(since

m_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
). In other words,
b_{k-1}=b_{k}
.

Now, consider the matrix

\left(h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\left[k=i\right],\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

The
\left(k-1\right)
-st and
k
-th rows of this matrix are
identical
31
31

31

Proof.
For each
j\in\left[n\right]
, the

j
-th entry of the
\left(k-1\right)
-st row of this matrix is

\displaystyle h\left(\underbrace{\mu_{k-1}}_{=\mu_{k}}-\left(k-1\right)+j+\underbrace{\left[k=k-1\right]}_{=0},\ \ \underbrace{b_{k-1}}_{=b_{k}},\ \ 1-j\right)

\displaystyle=h\left(\underbrace{\mu_{k}-\left(k-1\right)+j+0}_{=\mu_{k}-k+j+1},\ \ b_{k},\ \ 1-j\right)=h\left(\mu_{k}-k+j+1,\ \ b_{k},\ \ 1-j\right),

whereas the
j
-th entry of the
k
-th row of this matrix is

\displaystyle h\left(\mu_{k}-k+j+\underbrace{\left[k=k\right]}_{=1},\ \ b_{k},\ \ 1-j\right)=h\left(\mu_{k}-k+j+1,\ \ b_{k},\ \ 1-j\right).

These two entries are clearly equal. Thus, the
\left(k-1\right)
-st and

k
-th rows of this matrix are identical.
. Hence, this matrix has two equal
rows, so that its determinant vanishes. In other words,

\det\left(h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\left[k=i\right],\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}=0.

On the other hand,
\mu^{+k}
is not a partition (since
k\notin\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)
), so that
\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]=0
(by definition of
\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]
). Comparing this with the preceding equality, we obtain

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]=\det\left(h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\left[k=i\right],\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

Thus, Lemma
11.3

(a)
is proved in Case 2.

We have now proved Lemma
11.3

(a)
in both Cases 1 and
2, so that its proof is complete.

(b)
Assume that
m_{k}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
.

We are in one of the following two cases:

Case 1:
We have
k\in\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)
.

We have

.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case, we have
k\in\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)

\mu^{+k}

For each

\mathbf{b}^{\ast}=\left(b_{1}^{\ast},b_{2}^{\ast},b_{3}^{\ast},\ldots\right)

) (applied to

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]

\displaystyle=\det\left(h\left(\underbrace{\left(\mu^{+k}\right)_{i}}_{=\mu_{i}+\left[k=i\right]}-i+j,\ \ \underbrace{b_{i}^{\ast}}_{=b_{i}-\left[k=i\right]},\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}

\displaystyle=\det\left(h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\left[k=i\right],\ \ b_{i}-\left[k=i\right],\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

Thus, Lemma

Let us now consider Case 2. In this case, we have
k\notin\operatorname*{ER}\left(\mu\right)

\mu_{k-1}=\mu_{k}
. Thus, Lemma

-th rows of this matrix are
identical

32

32

Proof.

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]=\det\left(h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\left[k=i\right],\ \ b_{i}-\left[k=i\right],\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

Thus, Lemma
11.3

(b)
is proved in Case 2.

We have now proved Lemma
11.3

(b)
in both Cases 1 and
2, so that its proof is complete.

Proof 15.82

(Proof of Lemma
11.4
.)
.

Recall that
\mathbf{b}=\left(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},\ldots\right)
is the flagging induced by
\lambda/\mu
,
and that we have
\mu=\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{n}\right)
. Hence,
Corollary
8.18
yields

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu\right]=\det\left(\underbrace{h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j,\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i,j}\\
\text{(by the
definition of }u_{i,j}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}=\det\left(u_{i,j}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

This proves (
77
). However, the same
argument can be applied to
n-1
instead of
n
(since
\mu=\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{n-1}\right)
), and thus we obtain
(
78
).

Proof 15.83

(Proof of Lemma
11.5
.)
.

We first recall that every
i\in\left[n\right]
satisfies

\left[k=i\right]=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }i\neq k;\\
1,&\text{if }i=k.\end{cases}

Hence, every
i,j\in\left[n\right]
satisfy

\displaystyle u_{i,j+\left[k=i\right]}-p_{i}u_{i,j}\left[k=i\right]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}u_{i,j+0}-p_{i}u_{i,j}0,&\text{if }i\neq k;\\
u_{i,j+1}-p_{i}u_{i,j}1,&\text{if }i=k\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}u_{i,j},&\text{if }i\neq k;\\
u_{i,j+1}-p_{i}u_{i,j},&\text{if }i=k\end{cases}

(162)

(since
u_{i,j+0}-p_{i}u_{i,j}0=u_{i,j+0}=u_{i,j}
and
u_{i,j}1=u_{i,j}
).

We shall use the notation
A_{i,j}
for
the
\left(i,j\right)
-th entry of a matrix
A
.

We are in one of the following two cases:

Case 1:
We have
m_{k}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
.

Case 2:
We have
m_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case, we have
m_{k}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
. Thus, Lemma
11.3

(b)
yields

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]=\det\left(h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\left[k=i\right],\ \ b_{i}-\left[k=i\right],\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

(163)

Let
A
denote the matrix on the right hand side of this equality. Thus,
(
163
) rewrites as

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]=\det A.

(164)

We shall now compute the
\left(i,j\right)
-th entry

A_{i,j}=h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\left[k=i\right],\ \ b_{i}-\left[k=i\right],\ \ 1-j\right)

of the matrix
A
for any two numbers
i,j\in\left[n\right]
:

•

When
i\neq k
, we have
\left[k=i\right]=0
and thus

\displaystyle A_{i,j}

\displaystyle=h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\underbrace{\left[k=i\right]}_{=0},\ \ b_{i}-\underbrace{\left[k=i\right]}_{=0},\ \ 1-j\right)

\displaystyle=h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j,\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)=u_{i,j}

(165)

(by the definition of
u_{i,j}
).

•

When
i=k
, we have
\left[k=i\right]=1
and
b_{i}>0

33
33

33

Proof.
We have
i=k
, thus
m_{i}=m_{k}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)=\left\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\ldots\right\}
. Hence, there exists some
p\geq 1
such that
m_{i}=\ell_{p}
. Consider this
p
. Thus, Lemma
10.10
(applied to
p
instead of
j
)
yields
b_{i}=p\geq 1>0
.
, and thus

\displaystyle A_{i,j}

\displaystyle=h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\underbrace{\left[k=i\right]}_{=1},\ \ b_{i}-\underbrace{\left[k=i\right]}_{=1},\ \ 1-j\right)

\displaystyle=h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+1,\ \ b_{i}-1,\ \ 1-j\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+1,\ \ b_{i},\ \ -j\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i,j+1}\\
\text{(by the definition of }u_{i,j+1}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ -\left(x_{b_{i}}+\underbrace{y_{1-j}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since }1-j\leq 0\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right)\cdot\underbrace{h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j,\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i,j}\\
\text{(by the definition of }u_{i,j}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{by Corollary \ref{2hprop}, applied to }a=\mu_{i}-i+j+1\\
\text{and }b=b_{i}\text{ and }c=1-j\text{ (since }b_{i}>0\text{)}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=u_{i,j+1}-\underbrace{x_{b_{i}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=p_{i}\\
\text{(by the
definition of }p_{i}\text{,}\\
\text{since }i=k\text{ entails }m_{i}=m_{k}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}u_{i,j}

\displaystyle=u_{i,j+1}-p_{i}u_{i,j}.

(168)

Combining the formulas (
165
) and
(
168
), we conclude that the
\left(i,j\right)
-th entry of
A
always equals

A_{i,j}=\begin{cases}u_{i,j},&\text{if }i\neq k;\\
u_{i,j+1}-p_{i}u_{i,j},&\text{if }i=k\end{cases}\quad=u_{i,j+\left[k=i\right]}-p_{i}u_{i,j}\left[k=i\right]

(by (
162
)).
Hence,

A=\left(u_{i,j+\left[k=i\right]}-p_{i}u_{i,j}\left[k=i\right]\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

Therefore, (
164
) rewrites as

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]=\det\left(u_{i,j+\left[k=i\right]}-p_{i}u_{i,j}\left[k=i\right]\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

Thus, Lemma
11.5
is proved in Case 1.

Let us now consider Case 2. In this case, we have
m_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
. Thus, Lemma
11.3

(a)
yields

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]=\det\left(h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\left[k=i\right],\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

(169)

Let
A
denote the matrix on the right hand side of this equality. Thus,
(
169
) rewrites as

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]=\det A.

(170)

We shall now compute the
\left(i,j\right)
-th entry

A_{i,j}=h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\left[k=i\right],\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)

of the matrix
A
for any two numbers
i,j\in\left[n\right]
:

•

When
i\neq k
, we have
\left[k=i\right]=0
and thus

\displaystyle A_{i,j}

\displaystyle=h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\underbrace{\left[k=i\right]}_{=0},\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)

\displaystyle=h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j,\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)=u_{i,j}

(171)

(by the definition of
u_{i,j}
).

•

When
i=k
, we have
\left[k=i\right]=1
and thus

\displaystyle A_{i,j}

\displaystyle=h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+\underbrace{\left[k=i\right]}_{=1},\ \ b_{i},\ \ \underbrace{1-j}_{=-j+1}\right)

\displaystyle=h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+1,\ \ b_{i},\ \ -j+1\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j+1,\ \ b_{i},\ \ -j\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i,j+1}\\
\text{(by the definition of }u_{i,j+1}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\left(y_{\left(\mu_{i}-i+j\right)+b_{i}+\left(-j\right)+1}-\underbrace{y_{-j+1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since }-j+1\leq 0\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right)\cdot\underbrace{h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j,\ \ b_{i},\ \ -j+1\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=u_{i,j}\\
\text{(by the definition of }u_{i,j}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{by Corollary \ref{3hprop2},}\\
\text{applied to }a=\mu_{i}-i+j\text{ and }b=b_{i}\text{ and }c=-j\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=u_{i,j+1}+\underbrace{y_{\left(\mu_{i}-i+j\right)+b_{i}+\left(-j\right)+1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}\\
\text{(since }\left(\mu_{i}-i+j\right)+b_{i}+\left(-j\right)+1=\left(\mu_{i}-i\right)+1+b_{i}=m_{i}+1+b_{i}\\
\text{(because }\mu_{i}-i=m_{i}\text{))}\end{subarray}}\cdot\,u_{i,j}

\displaystyle=u_{i,j+1}+y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}\cdot u_{i,j}=u_{i,j+1}-\underbrace{\left(-y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=p_{i}\\
\text{(by the definition of
}p_{i}\text{,}\\
\text{since }i=k\text{ entails }m_{i}=m_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\,u_{i,j}

\displaystyle=u_{i,j+1}-p_{i}u_{i,j}.

(174)

Combining the formulas (
171
) and
(
174
), we conclude that the
\left(i,j\right)
-th entry of
A
always equals

A_{i,j}=\begin{cases}u_{i,j},&\text{if }i\neq k;\\
u_{i,j+1}-p_{i}u_{i,j},&\text{if }i=k\end{cases}\quad=u_{i,j+\left[k=i\right]}-p_{i}u_{i,j}\left[k=i\right]

(by (

)).
Hence,

A=\left(u_{i,j+\left[k=i\right]}-p_{i}u_{i,j}\left[k=i\right]\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

Therefore, (
170
) rewrites as

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]=\det\left(u_{i,j+\left[k=i\right]}-p_{i}u_{i,j}\left[k=i\right]\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

Thus, Lemma
11.5
is proved in Case 2.

We have now proved Lemma
11.5

Consequently, Lemma
11.5
always holds.

Proof 15.84

(Proof of Lemma
11.6
.)
.

We have

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu^{+k}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\det\left(u_{i,j+\left[k=i\right]}-p_{i}u_{i,j}\left[k=i\right]\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.k-th-det-1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(u_{i,j+\left[n=j\right]}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}-\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}p_{k}\right)\underbrace{\det\left(u_{i,j}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu\right]\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.lem.k-th-det-0.0}))}\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.det-uc}}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(u_{i,j+\left[n=j\right]}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}-\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}p_{k}\right)\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu\right].

This proves the lemma.

Proof 15.85

(Proof of Lemma
11.7
.)
.

For each
\ell\in\left[n-1\right]
,
we have
\underbrace{\mu_{n}}_{=0}-\,n+\ell=-n+\ell<0
(since
\ell\leq n-1<n
)
and
\left[n=\ell\right]=0
(since
\ell\leq n-1<n
and thus
n\neq\ell
).
Hence, for each
\ell\in\left[n-1\right]
, we have

\displaystyle u_{n,\ell+\left[n=\ell\right]}

\displaystyle=u_{n,\ell}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\ell+\underbrace{\left[n=\ell\right]}_{=0}=\ell\right)

\displaystyle=h\left(\mu_{n}-n+\ell,\ b_{n},\ 1-\ell\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }u_{n,\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of $h\left(a,b,c\right)$, since }\mu_{n}-n+\ell<0\right).

Therefore, we can apply Lemma
9.7
to
a_{i,j}=u_{i,j+\left[n=j\right]}
. We thus obtain

\det\left(u_{i,j+\left[n=j\right]}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}=u_{n,n+\left[n=n\right]}\cdot\det\left(u_{i,j+\left[n=j\right]}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n-1\right]}.

(175)

However,
\left[n=n\right]=1
, so that

\displaystyle u_{n,n+\left[n=n\right]}

\displaystyle=u_{n,n+1}

\displaystyle=h\left(\underbrace{\mu_{n}}_{=0}-\,n+\left(n+1\right),\ \underbrace{b_{n}}_{=n},\ \underbrace{1-\left(n+1\right)}_{=-n}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }u_{n,n+1}\right)

\displaystyle=h\left(\underbrace{-n+\left(n+1\right)}_{=1},\ n,\ -n\right)=h\left(1,\ n,\ -n\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+\sum\limits_{j=-n+1}^{-n+n}\underbrace{y_{j}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since }j\leq-n+n=0\text{, and}\\
\text{since }y_{i}=0\text{
for all }i\leq 0\text{)}\end{subarray}}\qquad\qquad\left(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.h1bc}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{j=-n+1}^{-n+n}0}_{=0}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}.

(176)

Moreover, for every
i,j\in\left[n-1\right]
, we have
j\neq n
(since

j\leq n-1<n
) and thus
\left[j=n\right]=0
, so that

u_{i,j+\left[n=j\right]}=u_{i,j+0}=u_{i,j}.

Hence,

\left(u_{i,j+\left[n=j\right]}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n-1\right]}=\left(u_{i,j}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n-1\right]}.

(177)

Using (
176
) and (
177
), we can
rewrite (
175
) as

\det\left(u_{i,j+\left[n=j\right]}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}=\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)\cdot\underbrace{\det\left(u_{i,j}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n-1\right]}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu\right]\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.lem.k-th-det-0.-1}))}\end{subarray}}=\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)\cdot\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu\right].

This proves Lemma
11.7
.

Proof 15.86

(Proof of Lemma
11.8
.)
.

From
\lambda_{n}=0
, we obtain
\lambda_{n+1}=0
.
Similarly,
\mu_{n+1}=0
. Thus, Lemma
10.11
can be applied.

We have

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}p_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\begin{cases}x_{b_{i}},&\text{if }m_{i}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right);\\
-y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}},&\text{if }m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{cases}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }p_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}x_{b_{i}}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}p_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}x_{b_{i}}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}x_{b_{i}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}x_{k}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.Deltas.x})}\end{subarray}}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}x_{k}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}.

This proves Lemma
11.8
.

Proof 15.88

(Proof of Lemma
12.1
.)
.

From
\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda\right)
, we obtain

j\leq\lambda_{i}
, so that
\lambda_{i}\geq j
. Hence, Lemma

5.3
(applied to
i
and
j
instead of
j
and
i
) yields

\lambda_{j}^{t}\geq i
. Now, the definition of
\ell_{i}
yields
\ell_{i}=\underbrace{\lambda_{i}}_{\geq j}-\,i\geq j-i
.
Furthermore, the definition
of
\ell^{t}_{j}
yields

\ell^{t}_{j}=\lambda^{t}_{j}-j
, so that

-\ell^{t}_{j}=-\left(\lambda^{t}_{j}-j\right)=j-\lambda^{t}_{j}
.
In other words,
j-\lambda^{t}_{j}=-\ell^{t}_{j}
.
Moreover, we thus obtain
-\ell^{t}_{j}=j-\underbrace{\lambda^{t}_{j}}_{\geq i}\leq j-i
.
Thus,

-\ell_{j}^{t}\leq j-i\leq\ell_{i}
(since
\ell_{i}\geq j-i
).

Next, we shall show that
n\geq\ell_{j}^{t}
.
Indeed, Lemma
5.3
(applied to
j
and
n
instead of
i
and
j
) shows that we have the equivalence
\left(\lambda^{t}_{j}\geq n\right)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\lambda_{n}\geq j\right)
.
Since the statement
\left(\lambda_{n}\geq j\right)
is false (because
\lambda_{n}=0<j
), we thus conclude that the statement
\left(\lambda^{t}_{j}\geq n\right)
is false as well.
In other words, we have
\lambda^{t}_{j}<n
.
However, the definition of
\ell_{j}^{t}
yields

\ell_{j}^{t}=\lambda_{j}^{t}-\underbrace{j}_{>0}<\lambda_{j}^{t}<n
.
Hence,
n\geq\ell_{j}^{t}
, so that
-n\leq-\ell_{j}^{t}
.

Now, the definition of
w_{k}
yields
w_{\ell_{i}}=\sum\limits_{k=-n}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}
and
w_{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}=\sum\limits_{k=-n}^{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}z_{k}
. Hence,

\displaystyle w_{\ell_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=-n}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=-n}^{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}z_{k}}_{=w_{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}}+\sum\limits_{k=-\ell_{j}^{t}}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }-n\leq-\ell_{j}^{t}\leq\ell_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=w_{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}+\sum\limits_{k=-\ell_{j}^{t}}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}.

In other words,

w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}=\sum\limits_{k=-\ell_{j}^{t}}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}.

(178)

Meanwhile, the equality (
6
) (with
c
,
i
and
j
renames as
\left(i,j\right)
,
k
and
p
) says that

h_{\lambda}\left(\left(i,j\right);z\right)=\sum\limits_{\left(k,p\right)\in H_{\lambda}\left((i,j)\right)}z_{p-k}.

(179)

Now, let us study the boxes
\left(k,p\right)
that belong to the hook

H_{\lambda}\left((i,j)\right)
. These are the boxes of
Y\left(\lambda\right)
that lie either in the
i
-th row to the east of
\left(i,j\right)
(including
\left(i,j\right)
itself), or in the
j
-th
column to the south of
\left(i,j\right)
. Thus, these boxes come in two types:

1.

The boxes
\left(k,p\right)\in H_{\lambda}\left((i,j)\right)

that belong to the
i
-th row (i.e., that satisfy
k=i
) are the boxes

\left(i,j\right),\ \left(i,j+1\right),\ \left(i,j+2\right),\ \ldots,\ \left(i,\lambda_{i}\right)

(since the
i
-th row of
Y\left(\lambda\right)
has
\lambda_{i}
boxes).

2.

The boxes
\left(k,p\right)\in H_{\lambda}\left((i,j)\right)

that do not belong to the
i
-th row (i.e., that satisfy
k\neq i
) are the
boxes

\left(i+1,j\right),\ \left(i+2,j\right),\ \left(i+3,j\right),\ \ldots,\ \left(\lambda_{j}^{t},j\right)

(since the
j
-th column of
Y\left(\lambda\right)
has
\lambda_{j}^{t}
boxes
(by (
18
), applied to
k=j
)).

Altogether, the boxes in
H_{\lambda}\left((i,j)\right)
are therefore the
boxes

\displaystyle\left(i,j\right),\ \left(i,j+1\right),\ \left(i,j+2\right),\ \ldots,\ \left(i,\lambda_{i}\right),

\displaystyle\left(i+1,j\right),\ \left(i+2,j\right),\ \left(i+3,j\right),\ \ldots,\ \left(\lambda_{j}^{t},j\right)

(and clearly, all these boxes are distinct). Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\left(k,p\right)\in H_{\lambda}\left((i,j)\right)}z_{p-k}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(z_{j-i}+z_{\left(j+1\right)-i}+z_{\left(j+2\right)-i}+\cdots+z_{\lambda_{i}-i}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=z_{j-i}+z_{\left(j-i\right)+1}+z_{\left(j-i\right)+2}+\cdots+z_{\lambda_{i}-i}\\
=\sum\limits_{k=j-i}^{\lambda_{i}-i}z_{k}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\underbrace{\left(z_{j-\left(i+1\right)}+z_{j-\left(i+2\right)}+z_{j-\left(i+3\right)}+\cdots+z_{j-\lambda_{j}^{t}}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=z_{\left(j-i\right)-1}+z_{\left(j-i\right)-2}+\cdots+z_{j-\lambda_{j}^{t}}\\
=\sum\limits_{k=j-\lambda_{j}^{t}}^{j-i-1}z_{k}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=j-i}^{\lambda_{i}-i}z_{k}+\sum\limits_{k=j-\lambda_{j}^{t}}^{j-i-1}z_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=j-i}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}+\sum\limits_{k=-\ell_{j}^{t}}^{j-i-1}z_{k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }\lambda_{i}-i=\ell_{i}\text{ and
}j-\lambda_{j}^{t}=-\ell_{j}^{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=-\ell_{j}^{t}}^{j-i-1}z_{k}+\sum\limits_{k=j-i}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}=\sum\limits_{k=-\ell_{j}^{t}}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }-\ell_{j}^{t}\leq j-i\leq\ell_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.lem.pf-hlf.w-w.1})}\right).

Hence, we can rewrite (
179
) as

h_{\lambda}\left(\left(i,j\right);z\right)=w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}.

This proves Lemma
12.1
.

Proof 15.89

(Proof of Lemma
12.2
.)
.

We are in one of the following two cases:

Case 1:
We have
i\leq n
.

Case 2:
We have
i>n
.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case,
i\leq n
. Thus,
m_{i}=\underbrace{\mu_{i}}_{\geq 0}-\underbrace{i}_{\leq n}\geq-n
.

Since
\lambda\supseteq\mu
, we have
\lambda_{i}\geq\mu_{i}
, and thus

\ell_{i}\geq m_{i}
.

However, the definition of the
w_{k}
yields
w_{\ell_{i}}=\sum\limits_{k=-n}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}
and
w_{m_{i}}=\sum\limits_{k=-n}^{m_{i}}z_{k}
. Hence,

\displaystyle w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{m_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=-n}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}-\sum\limits_{k=-n}^{m_{i}}z_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=m_{i}+1}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since
}\ell_{i}\geq m_{i}\geq-n\right).

(180)

However, the integers
j\geq 1
satisfying
\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)
are exactly the integers
j
such that
\mu_{i}<j\leq\lambda_{i}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq 1;\\
\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\end{subarray}}z_{j-i}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=\mu_{i}+1}^{\lambda_{i}}z_{j-i}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=\mu_{i}-i+1}^{\lambda_{i}-i}z_{k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }k\\
\text{for }j-i\text{ in the sum}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=m_{i}+1}^{\ell_{i}}z_{k}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since
}\mu_{i}-i=m_{i}\text{ and }\lambda_{i}-i=\ell_{i}\right).

Comparing this with (
180
), we obtain
w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{m_{i}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq 1;\\
\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\end{subarray}}z_{j-i}
. Hence, Lemma
12.2
is proved in
Case 1.

Let us now consider Case 2. In this case,
i>n
. Thus, both
\lambda_{i}
and

\mu_{i}
equal
0
(by the definition of
n
), so that we have
\lambda_{i}=\mu_{i}
,
and therefore we have

\ell_{i}=m_{i}
(since
\ell_{i}=\lambda_{i}-i
and
m_{i}=\mu_{i}-i
). Hence,
w_{\ell_{i}}=w_{m_{i}}
, so that
w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{m_{i}}=0
. On the other hand, the diagram
Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)

has no boxes in the
i
-th row (since
\lambda_{i}=0
), so that the sum

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq 1;\\
\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\end{subarray}}z_{j-i}
is empty and thus equals
0
. Comparing this with
w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{m_{i}}=0
, we obtain
w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{m_{i}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq 1;\\
\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\end{subarray}}z_{j-i}
. Thus,
Lemma
12.2
is proved in Case 2.

We have now proved Lemma
12.2
in both possible cases.

Proof 15.90

(Proof of Lemma
12.3
.)
.

Lemma
10.9
(applied to

f\left(i\right)=w_{m_{i}}
and
g\left(j\right)=w_{\ell_{j}}
)
yields

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n];\\
m_{i}\in\Delta(\lambda)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[n];\\
\ell_{j}\in\Delta(\mu)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{j}}

(181)

(since
w_{m_{i}}=w_{\ell_{j}}
whenever
m_{i}=\ell_{j}
).

From
\lambda_{n}=0
, we see that every
\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)
satisfies
i<n
and thus
i\leq n
and therefore
i\in\left[n\right]
. Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}z_{j-i}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\left[n\right]}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq 1;\\
\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\end{subarray}}z_{j-i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{m_{i}}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{section14.wY})}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{i\in\left[n\right]}\left(w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{m_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i\in\left[n\right]}w_{\ell_{i}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{i}\in\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{i}}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{i}}}-\underbrace{\sum\limits_{i\in\left[n\right]}w_{m_{i}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{i}\in\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{i}}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{i}}\right)-\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{i}\in\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{i}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[n];\\
\ell_{j}\in\Delta(\mu)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{j}}}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{i}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{k}}}-\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[n];\\
\ell_{j}\in\Delta(\mu)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{j}}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.lem.pf-hlf.4.1}))}\end{subarray}}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[n];\\
\ell_{j}\in\Delta(\mu)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{j}}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{k}}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[n];\\
\ell_{j}\in\Delta(\mu)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{j}}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{k}}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}.

This proves Lemma
12.3
.

Proof 15.91

(Proof of Lemma
12.4
.)
.

Let
\mathbf{b}=\left(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},\ldots\right)
be the flagging induced by
\lambda/\mu
.
(This was introduced in Definition
6.7
.)

Recall that
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
and
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots
have been indeterminates so far. But now let us instead set

x_{i}:=w_{\ell_{i}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ y_{i}:=-w_{-\ell_{i}^{t}-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }i\geq 1.

Thus, for any
\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\underbrace{x_{i}}_{=w_{\ell_{i}}}+\underbrace{y_{j}}_{=-w_{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}}

\displaystyle=w_{\ell_{i}}+\left(-w_{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}\right)=w_{\ell_{i}}-w_{-\ell_{j}^{t}-1}

\displaystyle=h_{\lambda}\left(\left(i,j\right);z\right)

(182)

(by Lemma
12.1
).

Now, Lemma
11.9
yields

\displaystyle\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}x_{k}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}\right)\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\nu\right]

(183)

(where the sum on the right hand side ranges over all partitions
\nu
that
satisfy
\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda
). However, for each
k\in\left[n\right]
satisfying
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)
, we have

x_{k}=w_{\ell_{k}}
(by the definition of
x_{k}
). Thus,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}x_{k}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{k}}.

(184)

Furthermore, for each
i\in\left[n\right]
satisfying
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
, we have
m_{i}+1+b_{i}\geq 1
and
\ell_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}^{t}=-1-m_{i}
(both by Lemma
10.12
) and

\displaystyle y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}

\displaystyle=-w_{-\ell_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}^{t}-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=-w_{-\left(-1-m_{i}\right)-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since
}\ell_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}^{t}=-1-m_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=-w_{m_{i}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }-\left(-1-m_{i}\right)-1=m_{i}\right).

Hence,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-w_{m_{i}}\right)=-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}.

Adding this equality to the equality (
184
), we find

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}x_{k}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{k}}+\left(-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}w_{\ell_{k}}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}w_{m_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}z_{j-i}

(185)

(by Lemma
12.3
).

Furthermore, if
\nu
is any partition, then the definition of

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\nu\right]
yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\nu\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{D\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in D}\underbrace{\left(x_{i}+y_{j}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=h_{\lambda}\left(\left(i,j\right);z\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.rec.2.x+y=h}),}\\
\text{since }\left(i,j\right)\in D\subseteq Y\left(\lambda\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{D\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in D}h_{\lambda}\left(\left(i,j\right);z\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{c\in E}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have renamed the}\\
\text{indices }D\text{ and }\left(i,j\right)\text{ as }E\text{ and }c\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\dfrac{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}

(188)

(since
\prod\limits_{c\in E}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)=\dfrac{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}
for
every subset
E
of
Y\left(\lambda\right)
).

In particular, applying this to
\nu=\mu
, we obtain

\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu\right]=\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\dfrac{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}.

(189)

Now, using the equalities (
185
), (
188
) and
(
189
), we can rewrite (
183
) as

\displaystyle\left(\sum\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}z_{j-i}\right)\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\dfrac{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}=\sum\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda}\mathbf{\ \ }\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\dfrac{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}.

Dividing both sides of this equality by
\left(\sum\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}z_{j-i}\right)\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)

(this is allowed, since
\mu\neq\lambda
ensures that
\sum\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}z_{j-i}\neq 0
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\dfrac{1}{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}=\dfrac{1}{\sum\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}z_{j-i}}\cdot\sum\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda}\mathbf{\ \ }\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\dfrac{1}{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}.

We can rewrite this as

\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}=\frac{1}{\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Y(\lambda/\mu)}z_{j-i}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda}\ \ \sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}

(since
\dfrac{1}{\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}=\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}
for every diagram
E
). Thus,
Lemma
12.4
is proved.

Proof 15.92

(Proof of Theorem
3.1
.)
.

We induct on
\left|Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\right|
:

Base case:
Assume that
\left|Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\right|=0
. Thus,
\mu=\lambda
(since
\lambda\supseteq\mu
by
assumption), so that the set
\operatorname*{SYT}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)

consists of a single tableau
T
(with no entries), and this tableau
T

satisfies
\mathbf{z}_{T}=1
. Hence,
\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname*{SYT}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\mathbf{z}_{T}=1
. On the other hand, from
\mu=\lambda
, we obtain

\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\lambda\right)=\left\{Y\left(\lambda\right)\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem.exc.equal}}\right),

and thus

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}=\prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus Y\left(\lambda\right)}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}=\left(\text{empty product}\right)=1.

Comparing this with
\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname*{SYT}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\mathbf{z}_{T}=1
, we find

\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname*{SYT}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\mathbf{z}_{T}=\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}.

Hence, Theorem
3.1
is proved in the case when
\left|Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\right|=0
. The base case is thus finished.

Induction step:
Fix a positive integer
N
. Assume (as the induction
hypothesis) that Theorem
3.1
holds for
\left|Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\right|=N-1
. We now fix a skew partition

\lambda/\mu
with
\left|Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\right|=N
.
Our goal is to prove that Theorem
3.1
holds for this
\lambda/\mu
.

From
\left|Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\right|=N>0
, we obtain

\mu\neq\lambda
. Hence, Lemma
12.4
yields

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Y(\lambda/\mu)}z_{j-i}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda}\ \ \sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}.

(190)

On the other hand, Lemma
4.4

(b)
shows that

\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname*{SYT}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\mathbf{z}_{T}=\frac{1}{\sum\limits\limits_{(i,j)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}z_{j-i}}\cdot\sum\limits\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda}\ \ \sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname*{SYT}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\mathbf{z}_{T}.

(191)

Now, if
\nu
is any partition satisfying
\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda
,
then
\left|Y\left(\lambda/\nu\right)\right|=\left|Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\right|-1
(since
\mu\lessdot\nu
entails that the
diagram
Y\left(\nu\right)
has exactly one more box than
Y\left(\mu\right)
, and thus
Y\left(\lambda/\nu\right)
has one fewer box
than
Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)
), and thus
\left|Y\left(\lambda/\nu\right)\right|=\underbrace{\left|Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)\right|}_{=N}-1=N-1
, which allows us to apply our induction
hypothesis to
\nu
instead of
\mu
. As a consequence, we see that any such
partition
\nu
satisfies

\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname*{SYT}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\mathbf{z}_{T}=\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}.

Hence, we can rewrite (
191
) as

\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname*{SYT}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\mathbf{z}_{T}=\frac{1}{\sum\limits\limits_{(i,j)\in Y\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}z_{j-i}}\cdot\sum\limits\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda}\ \ \sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\nu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}.

Comparing this with (
190
), we obtain

\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname*{SYT}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\mathbf{z}_{T}=\sum\limits_{E\in\mathcal{E}\left(\lambda/\mu\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{c\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\setminus E}\dfrac{1}{h_{\lambda}\left(c;z\right)}.

In other words, Theorem
3.1
holds for our
\lambda/\mu
. This
completes the induction step, and thus Theorem
3.1
is proved.

15.11
To Section
13
and Theorem
5.8

Proof 15.93

(Proof of Lemma
13.1
.)
.

We are in one of the following two cases:

Case 1:
We have
\lambda_{j}\geq i
.

Case 2:
We have
\lambda_{j}<i
.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case, we have
\lambda_{j}\geq i
. Thus,
Lemma
5.3
yields
\lambda_{i}^{t}\geq j
. Hence,

\underbrace{\lambda_{j}}_{\geq i}+\underbrace{\lambda_{i}^{t}}_{\geq j}-\,i-j\geq i+j-i-j=0>-1
, so that
\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{i}^{t}-i-j\neq-1
.
This proves Lemma
13.1
in Case 1.

Let us now consider Case 2. In this case, we have
\lambda_{j}<i
. Hence, we
don’t have
\lambda_{j}\geq i
. According to Lemma
5.3
,
this shows that we don’t have
\lambda_{i}^{t}\geq j
either. Hence, we have

\lambda_{i}^{t}<j
, so that
\lambda_{i}^{t}\leq j-1
(since
\lambda_{i}^{t}
and
j
are integers). Now,
\underbrace{\lambda_{j}}_{<i}+\underbrace{\lambda_{i}^{t}}_{\leq j-1}-\,i-j<i+j-1-i-j=-1
, so that

\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{i}^{t}-i-j\neq-1
. This proves Lemma

13.1
in Case 2.

Lemma
13.1
has now been proved in both cases, and thus
always holds.

Proof 15.94

(Proof of Lemma
13.2
.)
.

Well-known and easy consequence of the definition of
\lambda^{t}
.

Proof 15.95

(Proof of Lemma
13.3
.)
.

Assume the contrary. Thus,
-1-p\in\Delta\left(\lambda^{t}\right)
.

We have
p\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)=\left\{\lambda_{i}-i\ \mid\ i\geq 1\right\}
(by the definition of
\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
). In other words,
p=\lambda_{j}-j
for some
j\geq 1
. Consider this
j
.

Moreover,
-1-p\in\Delta\left(\lambda^{t}\right)=\left\{\lambda^{t}_{i}-i\ \mid\ i\geq 1\ldots\right\}
(by the definition of
\Delta\left(\lambda^{t}\right)
). In other words,
-1-p=\lambda^{t}_{i}-i
for some
i\geq 1
. Consider this
i
.

Adding the equalities
p=\lambda_{j}-j
and
-1-p=\lambda^{t}_{i}-i
together, we obtain

p+\left(-1-p\right)=\left(\lambda_{j}-j\right)+\left(\lambda^{t}_{i}-i\right)=\lambda_{j}+\lambda^{t}_{i}-i-j\neq-1

(by Lemma
13.1
). But this contradicts the obvious equality
p+\left(-1-p\right)=-1
.
Hence, our assumption was wrong, and Lemma
13.3
is proved.

Proof 15.96

(Proof of Lemma
13.4
.)
.

This is just Lemma
10.8
, with the letters
\lambda
,
\mu
,
\ell_{j}
and
k
renamed as
\mu
,
\lambda
,
m_{j}
and
i
.

Proof 15.97

(Proof of Lemma
13.5
.)
.

We shall proceed in multiple steps.

1.

The set
\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}
is finite.

[
Proof:
Pick an
n\in\mathbb{N}
that is large enough that

\lambda=\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\right)
(that
is,
\lambda_{n+1}=0
) and
\mu=\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{n}\right)
(that is,
\mu_{n+1}=0
). (Such an
n
exists, since
\lambda
and
\mu
are
partitions.) Then, Lemma

13.4
shows that every positive integer
i
that satisfies

m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
must satisfy
i\in\left[n\right]
. In other words,
\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}\subseteq\left[n\right]
.
Hence, the set
\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}
is finite (since

\left[n\right]
is finite).]

2.

If
i
is a positive integer satisfying
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
, then
m_{i}+1+b_{i}
is a positive integer
p
satisfying

\ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)
.

[
Proof:
Let
i
be a positive integer satisfying
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
. Lemma
10.12
then
shows that
m_{i}+1+b_{i}\geq 1
and
\ell_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}^{t}=-1-m_{i}
. From

m_{i}+1+b_{i}\geq 1
, we see that
m_{i}+1+b_{i}
is a positive integer.
Moreover, recall that
\Delta\left(\mu\right)=\left\{m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},\ldots\right\}
, so that
m_{i}\in\Delta\left(\mu\right)
. Hence, Lemma
13.3

(applied to
\mu
and
m_{i}
instead of
\lambda
and
p
) yields that

-1-m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)
. Therefore,
\ell_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}^{t}=-1-m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)
. Hence,

m_{i}+1+b_{i}
is a positive integer
p
satisfying
\ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)
.]

3.

The map

\displaystyle\Phi:\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left\{p\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\},

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\mapsto m_{i}+1+b_{i}

is well-defined.

[
Proof:
This is saying that if
i
is a positive integer satisfying

m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
, then
m_{i}+1+b_{i}
is a
positive integer
p
satisfying
\ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)
. But we just showed this in the previous step.]

4.

This map
\Phi
is furthermore injective.

[
Proof:
Let
u
and
v
be two distinct elements of
\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}
that
satisfy
\Phi\left(u\right)=\Phi\left(v\right)
. We must show that

u=v
.

The definition of
\Phi
yields
\Phi\left(u\right)=m_{u}+1+b_{u}
.
However, we have
u\in\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}
, so that
u\geq 1
and
m_{u}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
. Hence, Lemma
10.12
(applied to

i=u
) shows that
m_{u}+1+b_{u}\geq 1
and
\ell_{m_{u}+1+b_{u}}^{t}=-1-m_{u}
. In view of
\Phi\left(u\right)=m_{u}+1+b_{u}
, we can rewrite these
facts as
\Phi\left(u\right)\geq 1
and
\ell_{\Phi\left(u\right)}^{t}=-1-m_{u}
. Similarly, we find
\Phi\left(v\right)\geq 1
and

\ell_{\Phi\left(v\right)}^{t}=-1-m_{v}
.

Now, consider the two equalities
\ell_{\Phi\left(u\right)}^{t}=-1-m_{u}

and
\ell_{\Phi\left(v\right)}^{t}=-1-m_{v}
. Their left hand sides are
equal, since
\Phi\left(u\right)=\Phi\left(v\right)
. Thus, their
right hand sides are equal as well. In other words,
-1-m_{u}=-1-m_{v}
, so
that
m_{u}=m_{v}
.

However, Lemma
10.1
yields
m_{1}>m_{2}>m_{3}>\cdots
. In particular, the numbers
m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},\ldots
are
distinct. Hence, from
m_{u}=m_{v}
, we obtain
u=v
.

This completes the proof of the injectivity of
\Phi
.]

5.

We have
\left|\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}\right|\leq\left|\left\{p\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\}\right|
.

[
Proof:
We have just shown that there is an injective map (namely,

\Phi
) from the set
\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}
to the set
\left\{p\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\}
. Thus, the size of the
former set is at most as large as the size of the latter. In other words,

\left|\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}\right|\leq\left|\left\{p\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\}\right|
.]

6.

The sets
\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}
and
\left\{p\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\}
are finite and have the same size.

[
Proof:
We have just shown that

\displaystyle\left|\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left\{p\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{i}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\}\right|

(192)

(here, we renamed the index
p
as
i
). But we can apply the same reasoning
to the partitions
\mu^{t}
and
\lambda^{t}
instead of
\lambda
and
\mu
.
As a result, we obtain

\left|\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{i}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\right\}\right|\leq\left|\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}^{tt}\notin\Delta\left(\left(\lambda^{t}\right)^{t}\right)\right\}\right|,

where we set
m_{i}^{tt}=\left(\mu^{t}\right)_{i}^{t}-i
for each
i\geq 1
. This can be simplified to

\left|\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{i}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\right\}\right|\leq\left|\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}\right|

(since Lemma
13.2
yields
\left(\lambda^{t}\right)^{t}=\lambda
and
\left(\mu^{t}\right)^{t}=\mu
, so that
m_{i}^{tt}=\underbrace{\left(\mu^{t}\right)_{i}^{t}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mu_{i}\\
\text{(since }\left(\mu^{t}\right)^{t}=\mu\text{)}\end{subarray}}-i=\mu_{i}-i=m_{i}
for every
i\geq 1
). Combining this inequality with the
inequality (
192
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{i}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{p\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\}\right|.

Thus, the sets
\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}
and
\left\{p\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\}
have the same size. Since the first
of them is finite (by Step 1 above), we conclude that they are both are finite.]

7.

The map
\Phi
is bijective.

[
Proof:
The sets
\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}
and
\left\{p\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\}
are finite and have the same
size (as we have just seen), and the map
\Phi
between these sets is
injective (by Step 4 above). It is known that any injective map between two
finite sets of the same size must be bijective. Applying this to the map

\Phi
, we thus conclude that
\Phi
is bijective.]

Thus, the map
\Phi
is a bijection. This proves Lemma
13.5

(since the map
\Phi
is exactly the map described in Lemma
13.5
).

Proof 15.98

(Proof of Lemma
13.6
.)
.

Lemma
13.4
shows that every positive integer
i
that satisfies

m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
must satisfy
i\in\left[n\right]
. Hence, the summation sign
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq 1;\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}
is equivalent to
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}
. Therefore,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq 1;\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}.

(193)

On the other hand, Lemma
13.5
shows that the map

\displaystyle\left\{i\geq 1\ \mid\ m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left\{p\geq 1\ \mid\ \ell_{p}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\right\},

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\mapsto m_{i}+1+b_{i}

is a bijection. Hence, we can substitute
k
for
m_{i}+1+b_{i}
in the sum

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq 1;\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}
. We thus obtain

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq 1;\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1;\\
\ell_{k}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\end{subarray}}y_{k}.

Comparing this with
(
193
), we obtain

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1;\\
\ell_{k}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\end{subarray}}y_{k}.

This proves Lemma
13.6
.

Proof 15.99

(Proof of Theorem
5.8
.)
.

If
k
is a positive integer that
satisfies
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)
, then
k\in\left[n\right]
(by Lemma
10.8
). Thus, the summation
sign “
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}
” is equivalent to the
summation sign “
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1;\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}
”. Hence,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}x_{k}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1;\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}x_{k}.

(194)

Lemma
13.6
yields

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1;\\
\ell_{k}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\end{subarray}}y_{k}.

(195)

Now, Lemma
11.9
yields

\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\left[n\right];\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}x_{k}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\left[n\right];\\
m_{i}\notin\Delta\left(\lambda\right)\end{subarray}}y_{m_{i}+1+b_{i}}\right)\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu\right]=\sum\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\nu\right].

In view of (
194
) and (
195
), we can
rewrite this as

\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1;\\
\ell_{k}\notin\Delta\left(\mu\right)\end{subarray}}x_{k}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1;\\
\ell_{k}^{t}\notin\Delta\left(\mu^{t}\right)\end{subarray}}y_{k}\right)\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\mu\right]=\sum\limits_{\mu\lessdot\nu\subseteq\lambda}\mathbf{s}_{\lambda}\left[\nu\right].

This proves Theorem
5.8
.

16
Appendix: Odds and ends

In this short section, we shall discuss how a few of the auxiliary results
shown above can be extended or generalized.

16.1
To Section
8

The most general result in Section
8
is Theorem

8.3
. We can generalize it further by replacing the partition

\mu
by a skew partition
\mu/\nu
and adding a second flagging
\mathbf{a}
.
Here are the relevant definitions:

Definition 16.1
.

Let
\mu/\nu
be a skew partition.

(a)

A
semistandard tableau
of shape
\mu/\nu
is
defined just like a semistandard tableau of shape
\mu
(Definition

6.1
), but with each “
\mu
” replaced by “
\mu/\nu
”.

(b)

Let
\mathbf{a}=\left(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},\ldots\right)

and
\mathbf{b}=\left(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},\ldots\right)
be two flaggings (i.e., sequences of positive integers). A
semistandard tableau
T
of shape
\mu/\nu
is said to be
\mathbf{b}/\mathbf{a}
-flagged
if and only if it satisfies

a_{i}\leq T\left(i,j\right)\leq b_{i}\qquad\text{for all }\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\mu/\nu\right)

(that is, all entries in row
i
are
\geq a_{i}
and
\leq b_{i}
).

We let
\operatorname{FSSYT}\left(\mu/\nu,\ \mathbf{b}/\mathbf{a}\right)

be the set of all
\mathbf{b}/\mathbf{a}
-flagged semistandard tableaux of
shape
\mu/\nu
.

Now we can generalize Theorem
8.3
as follows:

Theorem 16.2
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. Let
u_{i,j}
be an
element of
R
for each pair
\left(i,j\right)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
. For each
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
and
r,q,d\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define
an element
h_{a,\ b;\ r,\ q}\left[d\right]\in R
by

h_{a,\ b;\ r,\ q}\left[d\right]:=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{r+1},i_{r+2},\ldots,i_{q}\right)\in\left[a,b\right]^{q-r};\\
i_{r+1}\leq i_{r+2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{q}\end{subarray}}\ \ \prod\limits_{j=r+1}^{q}u_{i_{j},\ j-d}

(where
\left[a,b\right]:=\left\{a,a+1,a+2,\ldots,b\right\}
). This
sum is understood to be
0
if
q<r
, and to be
1
if
q=r
.

Let
\mu=\left(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{n}\right)
and
\nu=\left(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n}\right)
be two partitions such that

\nu\subseteq\mu
. Let
\mathbf{a}=\left(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},\ldots\right)

and
\mathbf{b}=\left(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},\ldots\right)
be two weakly
increasing flaggings. Then,

\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname{FSSYT}\left(\mu/\nu,\ \mathbf{b}/\mathbf{a}\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\mu/\nu\right)}u_{T\left(i,j\right),\ j-i}=\det\left(h_{a_{j},\ b_{i};\ \nu_{j},\ \mu_{i}-i+j}\left[j\right]\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

The proof of Theorem
16.2
is mostly analogous to our
proof of Theorem
8.3
, with some occasional complications due
to the
a_{i}\leq T\left(i,j\right)
conditions and due to the presence of

\nu
. Some changes need to be made in Definition
8.4
: In
the definition of a legitimate permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
, the inequality

\mu_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-\sigma\left(i\right)+i\geq 0
must be
replaced by
\mu_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-\sigma\left(i\right)+i\geq\nu_{i}
. In the definition of
P\left(\sigma\right)
, the inequality

j\leq\mu_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-\sigma\left(i\right)+i
must be
replaced by
\nu_{i}<j\leq\mu_{\sigma\left(i\right)}-\sigma\left(i\right)+i
. The
\mathbf{b}
-flagged
\sigma
-arrays should be replaced by
“
\mathbf{b}/\mathbf{a}
-flagged
\sigma
-arrays”, which are defined similarly but require that every
entry of
T
in the
i
-th row is
\leq b_{\sigma\left(i\right)}
and

\geq a_{i}
(note the different subscripts!). The definition of an outer
failure (Definition
8.9

(a)
) should be adapted by
replacing “
\left(i-1,j\right)\notin P\left(\sigma\right)
” by “
\left(i-1,j\right)\notin P\left(\sigma\right)\cup Y\left(\nu\right)
” (so that
\left(i,j\right)
does
not
count as a failure if

\nu_{i-1}\geq j
). We leave it to the reader to check that the argument (most
importantly, the proof of Lemma
8.16
) survives all these
changes (and some others that are forced by these).

16.2
To Section
9

As we said, Lemma
9.1
is just the
r=1
case of

[
MuiMet60
, §319]
. Here is the general case:

Lemma 16.3
.

Let
P
and
Q
be two
n\times n
-matrices over some commutative ring.

For each subset
K
of
\left[n\right]
, we let

P\underset{\operatorname*{row}}{\overset{K}{\leftarrow}}Q
denote the

n\times n
-matrix that is obtained from
P
by replacing the
k
-th row by
the
k
-th row of
Q
for all
k\in K
. (That is, the
\left(i,j\right)
-th entry of this matrix is
\begin{cases}P_{i,j},&\text{ if }i\notin K;\\
Q_{i,j},&\text{ if }i\in K\end{cases}
for every
i,j\in\left[n\right]
.)

For each subset
K
of
\left[n\right]
, we let

P\underset{\operatorname*{col}}{\overset{K}{\leftarrow}}Q
denote the

n\times n
-matrix that is obtained from
P
by replacing the
k
-th column by
the
k
-th column of
Q
for all
k\in K
. (That is, the
\left(i,j\right)
-th entry of this matrix is
\begin{cases}P_{i,j},&\text{ if }j\notin K;\\
Q_{i,j},&\text{ if }j\in K\end{cases}
for every
i,j\in\left[n\right]
.)

Let
r\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,n\right\}
. Then,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\subseteq\left[n\right];\\
\left|K\right|=r\end{subarray}}\det\left(P\underset{\operatorname*{row}}{\overset{K}{\leftarrow}}Q\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\subseteq\left[n\right];\\
\left|K\right|=r\end{subarray}}\det\left(P\underset{\operatorname*{col}}{\overset{K}{\leftarrow}}Q\right).

This can be proved in a similar way as we proved Lemma
9.1
,
but using Laplace expansion along multiple rows/columns (see, e.g.,

[
Grinbe20
, Theorem 6.156]
).

Finally, we note that Lemma
9.5
has an analogue in which
p_{i}

is replaced by
p_{j}
:

Lemma 16.4
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Let
R
be a commutative ring.

Let
u_{i,j}
be an element of
R
for each
i\in\left[n\right]
and each

j\in\left[n+1\right]
. Let
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{n}
be
n
further
elements of
R
.

Then,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\det\left(u_{i,j+\left[k=i\right]}-p_{j}u_{i,j}\left[k=i\right]\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}

\displaystyle=\det\left(u_{i,j+\left[n=j\right]}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}-\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}p_{k}\right)\det\left(u_{i,j}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

Proof 16.5

(Proof of Lemma
16.4
.)
.

Proceed exactly as in the above proof of Lemma
9.5
, replacing the
p_{j}
by
p_{i}
. The only nontrivial change is replacing the computation (
145
) by

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\ \ \sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{n}\left(-1\right)^{k+\ell}p_{\ell}u_{k,\ell}\det\left(U_{\sim k,\sim\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{n}p_{\ell}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\left(-1\right)^{k+\ell}\underbrace{u_{k,\ell}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=U_{k,\ell}\\
\text{(by the definition of
}U\text{)}\end{subarray}}\det\left(U_{\sim k,\sim\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{n}p_{\ell}\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\left(-1\right)^{k+\ell}U_{k,\ell}\det\left(U_{\sim k,\sim\ell}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\det U\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.lem.det-u.lapc}), applied to }A=U\text{)}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{n}p_{\ell}\det U=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}p_{k}\det U

\displaystyle=\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}p_{k}\right)\det\underbrace{U}_{=\left(u_{i,j}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}}=\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}p_{k}\right)\det\left(u_{i,j}\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

16.3
To Section
13

Finally, we note that Lemma
13.3
has a converse:

Proposition 16.6
.

Let
\lambda
be any partition. Let

p\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then,
p\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
if and only if

-1-p\notin\Delta\left(\lambda^{t}\right)
.

Proof 16.7

(Proof of Proposition
16.6
.)
.

The “only if” direction was Lemma
13.3
. It
remains to prove the “if” direction. Thus,
we assume that
-1-p\notin\Delta\left(\lambda^{t}\right)
. Our goal is to
prove that
p\in\Delta\left(\lambda\right)
.

We will use Convention
10.1
. Set
\lambda_{0}:=\infty
and
\ell_{0}:=\infty
.
Thus, the equality
\ell_{i}=\lambda_{i}-i
holds not only for all
i\geq 1

(for which it follows from Convention
10.1
), but also for
all
i=0
. In other words,
\ell_{i}=\lambda_{i}-i
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Also,
\ell_{0}>\ell_{1}>\ell_{2}>\ell_{3}>\cdots
(this is proved as in the
proof of Lemma
10.13
above). Hence, there are only finitely many

i\in\mathbb{N}
that satisfy
\ell_{i}\geq p
. Moreover, there exists at
least one such
i
(namely,
i=0
, since
\ell_{0}=\infty\geq p
). Consider
the
largest
such
i
. Then,
\ell_{i}\geq p
but
\ell_{i+1}<p
.

We shall show that
\ell_{i}=p
.

Indeed, assume the contrary. Thus,
\ell_{i}\neq p
. Combining this with

\ell_{i}\geq p
, we obtain
\ell_{i}>p
. In other words,
\lambda_{i}-i>p

(since
\ell_{i}=\lambda_{i}-i
). In other words,
\lambda_{i}>i+p
. Hence,

\lambda_{i}\geq i+p+1
(since
\lambda_{i}
and
i+p
are integers or

\infty
).

Also, we have
\ell_{i+1}=\lambda_{i+1}-\left(i+1\right)=\lambda_{i+1}-i-1
and thus
\lambda_{i+1}-i-1=\ell_{i+1}<p
. In other words,

\lambda_{i+1}-i<p+1
. Since
\lambda_{i+1}-i
and
p+1
are integers, this
entails
\lambda_{i+1}-i\leq\left(p+1\right)-1=p
. Therefore,

\lambda_{i+1}\leq i+p<i+p+1
. Thus,
i+p+1>\lambda_{i+1}\geq 0
. This shows
that
i+p+1
is a positive integer.

Thus, Lemma
5.3
(applied to
i+p+1
and
i+1
instead of

i
and
j
) yields the logical equivalence

\left(\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}\geq i+1\right)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\lambda_{i+1}\geq i+p+1\right).

Since the statement
\left(\lambda_{i+1}\geq i+p+1\right)
is false
(because
\lambda_{i+1}<i+p+1
), we thus conclude that the statement
\left(\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}\geq i+1\right)
is also false. In other words,

\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}<i+1
. Hence,
\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}\leq i
(since both
sides of the inequality are integers).

Moreover, Lemma
5.3
(applied to
i+p+1
and
i
instead of

i
and
j
) yields the logical equivalence
34
34

34

To be precise, this
argument only works when
i>0
. However, in the remaining case, the conclusion
(
\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}\geq i
) is obvious anyway (since
\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}\geq 0
), so we don’t need this argument.

\left(\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}\geq i\right)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\lambda_{i}\geq i+p+1\right).

Since the statement
\lambda_{i}\geq i+p+1
holds, we thus conclude that

\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}\geq i
holds as well.

Combining
\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}\geq i
with
\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}\leq i
, we
find
\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}=i
. Now, the definition of
\ell_{i+p+1}^{t}
yields

\ell_{i+p+1}^{t}=\underbrace{\lambda_{i+p+1}^{t}}_{=i}-\left(i+p+1\right)=i-\left(i+p+1\right)=-1-p.

Hence,

\displaystyle-1-p

\displaystyle=\ell_{i+p+1}^{t}\in\left\{\ell_{1}^{t},\ell_{2}^{t},\ell_{3}^{t},\ldots\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }i+p+1\text{ is a
positive integer}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta\left(\lambda^{t}\right),

which contradicts
-1-p\notin\Delta\left(\lambda^{t}\right)
. This
contradiction shows that our assumption was false. Hence,
\ell_{i}=p
is proved.

Now,
i\neq 0
(since
\ell_{i}=p\neq\infty=\ell_{0}
) and thus
i\geq 1
.
Hence,
\ell_{i}\in\left\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\ldots\right\}
.
Thus, from
\ell_{i}=p
, we obtain

p=\ell_{i}\in\left\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\ldots\right\}=\Delta\left(\lambda\right),

which is precisely what we desired to prove. Thus, the proof of Proposition

16.6
is complete.

17
Appendix: Deriving Proposition
8.2
from the literature

In this short appendix, we shall briefly outline how Proposition
8.2
can be derived
from some known results.

17.1
Deriving Proposition
8.2
as a consequence of Gessel–Viennot

First, we shall show how Proposition
8.2
can be obtained
from
[
GesVie89
, Theorem 3]
.

Let us use the notations of
[
GesVie89
, Theorem 3]
, but set
b_{i}:=1
and

\mu:=\varnothing
and
k:=n
.
Furthermore, we define a labelling set

L:=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
(consisting of all possible boxes), a relabeling function

\mathbf{f}
given by
f_{r}\left(s\right):=\left(r,s\right)\in L
,
and a weight function
w
given by
w\left(r,s\right)=x_{s}+y_{s+r}
for
each
\left(r,s\right)\in L
(where we set
x_{i}=y_{i}=0
for all

i\leq 0
). Then,
w\left(f_{r}\left(s\right)\right)=x_{s}+y_{s+r}
for
any
r
and
s
. Now,
[
GesVie89
, Theorem 3]
says that

\displaystyle\left(\text{the sum of the weights of }\mathbf{f}\left(T\right)\text{
over all tableaux }T\text{ of shape }\lambda\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\text{satisfying }1\leq T_{i,j}\leq d_{i}\text{
for all }\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\lambda\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(H_{\mathbf{f}}\left(-i+1,\ 1,\ \lambda_{j}-j+1,\ d_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}.

(196)

However, the left hand side of this equality is easily seen to be exactly our

\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname{FSSYT}\left(\mu,\mathbf{b}\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\mu\right)}\left(x_{T\left(i,j\right)}+y_{T\left(i,j\right)+j-i}\right),

if we rename
\lambda
and
d_{i}
as
\mu
and
b_{i}
. On the other hand,
the right hand side of (
196
) (after the same renaming) becomes our

\det\left(h\left(\mu_{j}-j+i,\ \ b_{j},\ \ 1-i\right)\right)_{i,j\in[n]},

because every
i,j\in\left[n\right]
satisfy

\displaystyle H_{\mathbf{f}}\left(-i+1,\ 1,\ \lambda_{j}-j+1,\ d_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{1\leq n_{-i+1}\leq n_{-i+2}\leq\cdots\leq n_{\lambda_{j}-j}\leq d_{j}}w\left(f_{-i+1}\left(n_{-i+1}\right)\right)w\left(f_{-i+2}\left(n_{-i+2}\right)\right)\cdots w\left(f_{\lambda_{j}-j}\left(n_{\lambda_{j}-j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{1\leq n_{-i+1}\leq n_{-i+2}\leq\cdots\leq n_{\lambda_{j}-j}\leq d_{j}}\ \ \prod\limits_{g=-i+1}^{\lambda_{j}-j}\underbrace{w\left(f_{g}\left(n_{g}\right)\right)}_{=x_{n_{g}}+y_{n_{g}+g}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{1\leq n_{-i+1}\leq n_{-i+2}\leq\cdots\leq n_{\lambda_{j}-j}\leq d_{j}}\ \ \prod\limits_{g=-i+1}^{\lambda_{j}-j}\left(x_{n_{g}}+y_{n_{g}+g}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{1\leq m_{1}\leq m_{2}\leq\cdots\leq m_{\lambda_{j}-j+i}\leq d_{j}}\ \ \prod\limits_{g=1}^{\lambda_{j}-j+i}\left(x_{m_{g}}+y_{m_{g}+g-i}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have shifted
the indices by setting }m_{g}=n_{g-i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(m_{1},m_{2},\ldots,m_{\lambda_{j}-j+i}\right)\in\left[d_{j}\right]^{\lambda_{j}-j+i};\\
m_{1}\leq m_{2}\leq\cdots\leq m_{\lambda_{j}-j+i}\end{subarray}}\ \ \prod\limits_{g=1}^{\lambda_{j}-j+i}\left(x_{m_{g}}+y_{m_{g}+g-i}\right)

\displaystyle=h\left(\lambda_{j}-j+i,\ \ d_{j},\ \ 1-i\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{using our Definition \ref{defh}}\right).

Hence, the equality (
196
) becomes

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname{FSSYT}\left(\mu,\mathbf{b}\right)}\ \ \prod\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\mu\right)}\left(x_{T\left(i,j\right)}+y_{T\left(i,j\right)+j-i}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(h\left(\mu_{j}-j+i,\ \ b_{j},\ \ 1-i\right)\right)_{i,j\in[n]}

\displaystyle=\det\left(h\left(\mu_{i}-i+j,\ \ b_{i},\ \ 1-j\right)\right)_{i,j\in[n]}

(since
\det\left(A^{T}\right)=\det A
for any square matrix
A
).
Proposition
8.2
thus follows.

17.2
Deriving Proposition
8.2
from Chen–Li–Louck

In their study of flagged double Schur functions, Chen, Li and Louck have obtained a determinantal formula
[
ChLiLo02
, second bullet point after Theorem 4.2]
that can, too, be used to prove Proposition
8.2
.
We shall recall this formula, and then very tersely outline the derivation.

For any
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define an element
h_{a}\left(X_{b}/Y_{c}\right)
of
R
by

h_{a}\left(X_{b}/Y_{c}\right):=\left(\text{the coefficient of }t^{a}\text{ in the power series }\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{c}\left(1+y_{i}t\right)}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{b}\left(1-x_{j}t\right)}\in R\left[\left[t\right]\right]\right)

(understanding empty products as
1
as usual).
Then,
[
ChLiLo02
, second bullet point after Theorem 4.2]
(translated into our language, and upon the substitution
y_{j}\mapsto-y_{j}
) says that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{T\in\operatorname{FSSYT}(\mu,\mathbf{b})}\ \ \prod\limits_{\left(i,j\right)\in Y\left(\mu\right)}\left(x_{T\left(i,j\right)}+y_{T\left(i,j\right)+j-i}\right)=\det\left(h_{\mu_{i}-i+j}\left(X_{b_{i}}/Y_{\lambda_{i}+b_{i}-i}\right)\right)_{i,j\in\left[n\right]}

(with the notations of Theorem
8.2
).
In order to derive Proposition
8.2
from this, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle h\left(a,b,c\right)=h_{a}\left(X_{b}/Y_{a+b+c-1}\right)

(197)

for any
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying
c\leq 0
.
This equality (
197
) can, in turn, be derived again from
[
ChLiLo02
, second bullet point after Theorem 4.2]
(applied to
n=1
,
\lambda_{1}=a
and
b_{1}=b
). (To be more precise, this proves (
197
) for
c=0
, but then the general case follows by shifting the
y
-variables.)
